\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

It is defined by the pushout square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\{0,1\},0]}\cup_{[\{0\},\{0\}]}[0,\{0,1\}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1,1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[0,0]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}

of bisimplicial spaces.

Notation 4.11
.

If
\mathscr{P}
is a double Segal space, then we will write

C_{\mathscr{P}}\subset\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{PSh}(\Delta^{\times 2})}(L,\mathscr{P})

for the subspace spanned by the companionship units.

The remainder of this section is dedicated to proving the following two statements:

Theorem 4.12
.

Let
\mathscr{P}
be a double Segal space. Then restriction along
the canonical inclusion
L\rightarrow\mathfrak{comp}

induces an equivalence

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S})}(\mathfrak{comp},\mathscr{P})\xrightarrow{\simeq}C_{\mathscr{P}}.

Equivalently, if
\eta
is a companionship unit in
\mathscr{P}
, then the fiber

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S})}(\mathfrak{comp},\mathscr{P})_{\eta}=\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S})}(\mathfrak{comp},\mathscr{P})\times_{\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S})}(L,\mathscr{P})}\{\eta\}

is contractible.

Theorem 4.13
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{P}
is a double Segal space so that
\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})
is locally complete. Then the restriction arrow

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S})}(\mathfrak{comp},\mathscr{P})\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S})}([1]_{v},\mathscr{P})

is a monomorphism with image given by the subspace of vertical arrows of
\mathscr{P}
that admit a companion.

Remark 4.14
.

In the context of (strict) double categories,
4.13
can be recovered as a special
case of a theorem of Grandis and Paré
[
GP04
, Theorem 1.8]
.

One may formally deduce the following results by applying the transpose
(-)^{\mathrm{t}}
and horizontal opposite
(-)^{\mathrm{hop}}

dualities.

Corollary 4.15
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{P}
is a double Segal space so that
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})
is locally complete. Then the restriction arrow

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S})}(\mathfrak{comp},\mathscr{P})\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S})}([1]_{h},\mathscr{P})

is a monomorphism with image given by the subspace of horizontal arrows of
\mathscr{P}
that are a companion.

Corollary 4.16
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{P}
is a double Segal space. Then the following assertions holds:

•

if
\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})
is locally complete, then the restriction map

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S})}(\mathfrak{conj},\mathscr{P})\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S})}([1]_{v},\mathscr{P})

is a monomorphism with image given by the subspace of vertical arrows of
\mathscr{P}
that admit a conjoint,

•

if
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})
is locally complete, then the restriction map

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S})}(\mathfrak{conj},\mathscr{P})\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S})}([1]_{h},\mathscr{P})

is a monomorphism with image given by the subspace of horizontal arrows of
\mathscr{P}
that are a conjoint.

Remark 4.17
.

In particular, note that every locally complete double Segal space
\mathscr{P}
meets the conditions that are posed in
4.13
,

4.15
and
4.16
.

Corollary 4.18
.

The full subcategory spanned by the double Segal spaces
\mathscr{P}
for which:

•

\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})
is locally complete,

•

every vertical arrow of
\mathscr{P}
admits a companion,

is a reflective subcategory of
\mathrm{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S})
, and particular, closed under limits.

Proof.

On account of
4.13
, this is precisely the full subcategory of double Segal spaces
local with respect to the maps
[1;J]_{h}\rightarrow[1]_{h}
and
[1]_{v}\rightarrow\mathfrak{comp}
.
∎

Remark 4.19
.

In
[
SS86
]
, Schanuel and Street define the
free-living adjunction
(gaunt) 2-category

\mathfrak{adj},

and show
that every adjunction in a 2-category can be upgraded to a functor out of this 2-category. This result was then upgraded by Riehl–Verity to the
(\infty,2)
-categorical context
[
RV16
]
,
where
it is shown that every left adjoint arrow in an
(\infty,2)
-category uniquely (up to contractible choice) extends to a functor out of the free-living adjunction.

One may combine

4.5
and
4.16

to conclude that the
(\infty,2)
-category
obtained by applying the left adjoint
\mathrm{Gr}(-)
of the squares functor
\mathrm{Sq}:\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}\rightarrow\mathrm{DblCat}_{\infty}^{c}

to the free-living conjunction, carries the same universal property of
\mathfrak{adj}
exhibited by Riehl–Verity. Consequently, we deduce
that there is an equivalence

\mathrm{Gr}(\mathfrak{conj})\simeq\mathfrak{adj}.

In fact, one can write down an explicit functor
\mathfrak{conj}\rightarrow\mathrm{Sq}(\mathfrak{adj})
using
the description of the free-living adjunction of Schanuel–Street. If one succeeds to show that the adjunct map

\mathrm{Gr}(\mathfrak{conj})\rightarrow\mathfrak{adj}
is an equivalence — without reasoning with universal properties —
then
4.16
can be used to provide an independent proof of the main result of Riehl–Verity
[
RV16
]
.

4.2.
A special extension theorem

The main ingredient of the proof of
4.12
and

4.13

is a special extension result for double Segal spaces with respect to companionship units.
We first set up the necessary notation.

Notation 4.20
.

We will consider the quotient
L[n,m]
that is defined by the pushout square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\{0\},\{0\leq 1\}]}\cup_{[\{0\},\{0\}]}[\{0\leq\dotsb\leq n\},\{0\}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n,m]}.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[0,0]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L[n,m],}

of bisimplicial spaces, so that
L[1,1]=L
.

Notation 4.21
.

Let
k\geq 0
and suppose that
S
is a subset of
\{0,\dotsc,k\}
. Then we will use the notation of
[
Joy08
, Subsection 2.2.1]
and
consider the following simplicial subset

\textstyle\Lambda^{S}[k]:=\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i\in\{0,\dotsc,k\}\setminus S}d_{i}[k-1]\subset[k].

For
n,m\geq 0
and subsets
S
and
T
of
[n]
and
[m]
respectively, we will consider the bisimplicial subset

\Lambda^{S,T}[n,m]:=\Lambda^{S}[n]_{h}\times[m]_{v}\cup_{\Lambda^{S}[n]_{h}\times\Lambda^{T}[m]_{v}}[n]_{h}\times\Lambda^{T}[m]_{v}\subset[n,m].

Whenever
S\subset\{0,\dotsc,n-1\}
and
T\subset\{0,\dotsc,m-1\}
, we will consider the quotients

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\{0\},\{0\leq 1\}]}\cup_{[\{0\},\{0\}]}[\{0\leq\dotsb\leq n\},\{0\}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{S,T}[n,m]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[0,0]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{S,T}_{L}[n,m].}

For
n\geq 1
and
m\geq 2
, we have a map

L^{\sqcup n}\cong\{1,\dotsc,n\}\times L\rightarrow\Lambda^{S,T}_{L}[n,m]

where the restriction to
\{i\}\times L
factors through the
(1,1)
-bisimplex

[\{0\leq i\},\{0\leq 1\}]:\{i\}\times[1,1]\rightarrow\Lambda^{S,T}[n,m].

For brevity, we will also make use of the notation

\Gamma_{L}^{T}[n,m]:=\Lambda^{\emptyset,T}_{L}[n,m].

Let
\mathscr{P}
be a double Segal space, and
suppose that we are given a map
\sigma:\Gamma^{0}_{L}[n,m]\rightarrow\mathscr{P}
of bisimplicial spaces
with
n\geq 1
and
m\geq 2
. Moreover, we impose the condition that each restriction

\sigma|(\{i\}\times L):L\rightarrow\mathscr{P}

classifies a companionship unit for
i=1,\dotsc,n
.
The special extension theorem that we will prove, asserts that the dotted lift in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma^{0}_{L}[n,m]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L[n,m]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}

exists under these conditions, and that it is unique up to contractible choice. Here the left vertical arrow is the inclusion.

Before we state the theorem, let us sketch informally how one can produce such a lift for the minimal case that
n=1
and
m=2
. In this case,

\sigma
classifies a vertical arrow
k:y\rightarrow b
and two 2-cells

\eta=\hbox to74.98pt{\vbox to44.38pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 37.48837pt\lower-22.78194pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-37.48837pt}{-15.99307pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -51.87 -22.13)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.39998pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=2.33746pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -35.04 13.52 L -35.04 -10.83}{fill:none} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\pgfinnerlinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=1.5375pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -35.04 13.52 L -35.04 -10.83}{fill:none} }\lxSVG@closescope {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {}{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}

in
\mathscr{P}
. By assumption,
\eta
is a companionship unit and has an associated
counit
\epsilon
. The candidate lift
L[1,2]\rightarrow\mathscr{P}

classifies the diagram in
\mathscr{P}
that is obtained from the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\scriptstyle\alpha}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\scriptstyle\epsilon}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\scriptstyle\eta}

by horizontally pasting the columns (and the vertical boundaries of the subdivided 2-cell in the resulting second row).
One can check that this is indeed a lift by using the companionship triangle identities. The proof of the special extension theorem
for the case that
n=1
and
m=2
is essentially an elaboration of this idea.

Theorem 4.22
.

Let
\mathscr{P}
be a double Segal space.
Let
n\geq 1
and
m\geq 2
. Then the fibered map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{PSh}(\Delta^{\times 2})}(L[n,m],\mathscr{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{PSh}(\Delta^{\times 2})}(\Gamma^{0}_{L}[n,m],\mathscr{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{PSh}(\Delta^{\times 2})}(L,\mathscr{P})^{\times n}}

becomes an equivalence when pulled back to the subspace
C_{\mathscr{P}}^{\times n}
of
\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{PSh}(\Delta^{\times 2})}(L,\mathscr{P})^{\times n}
. In other words,
the map on fibers

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{PSh}(\Delta^{\times 2})}(L[n,m],\mathscr{P})_{\eta}\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{PSh}(\Delta^{\times 2})}(\Gamma^{0}_{L}[n,m],\mathscr{P})_{\eta}

is an equivalence for every tuple
\eta=(\eta_{1},\dotsc,\eta_{n})\in C_{\mathscr{P}}^{\times n}
.

Remark 4.23
.

There are more special extension or lifting results of a similar flavor in higher category theory:

•

Joyal’s lifting theorem

[
Joy02
, Theorem 2.2]
for quasi-categories with respect to equivalences,

•

a version of the preceding theorem for Segal spaces by Rezk
[
Rez01
, Lemma 11.10]
,

•

a result for quasi-categorically enriched categories by Riehl and Verity
[
RV16
, Proposition 4.3.6]
with respect to adjunction counits.

To prove
4.22
, we present the following reduction step:

Lemma 4.24
.

Suppose that
4.22
holds for
(n,m)=(1,2)
. Then it holds for all
n\geq 1
and
m\geq 2
.

In turn, we need the following ingredient to make this reduction:

Lemma 4.25
.

Let
n,m\geq 0
, and suppose that
S
and
T
are subsets of
[n]
and
[m]
respectively. Suppose that one of the complements

[n]\setminus S
or
[m]\setminus T
is not convex.
Then the inclusion
\Lambda^{S,T}[n,m]\rightarrow[n,m]
is contained in the saturation of (Seg).

Proof.

Without loss of generality, let us assume
[n]\setminus S
is not convex. It follows from
[
JT07
, Lemma 3.5]
and
[
Joy08
, Proposition 2.12]
that
\Lambda^{S}[n]_{h}\rightarrow[n]_{h}
is contained in the saturation
of (
Seg
). Thus
3.44
now implies that
\Lambda^{S}[n]_{h}\times[m]_{v}\rightarrow[n,m]
and

\Lambda^{S}[n]_{h}\times\Lambda^{T}[m]_{v}\rightarrow[n]_{h}\times\Lambda^{T}[m]_{v}
are contained in (
Seg
) as well. In turn,
this implies that
that the total composite

\Lambda^{S}[n]_{h}\times[m]_{v}\rightarrow\Lambda^{S,T}[n,m]\rightarrow[n,m]

as well as the left inclusion in the composite are contained in (
Seg
). So the desired result follows from 2-out-of-3.
∎

Proof of
4.24
.

The proof is by induction.
We will demonstrate that if
4.22
holds for a pair
(n,m)
with
n\geq 1
,
m\geq 2
, then it will hold for the succesive pairs
(n+1,m)
and
(n,m+1)
as well.

Let us start by treating the first pair. Note that we have inclusions

\Lambda^{\{1\},\{0\}}_{L}[n+1,m]\rightarrow\Gamma^{0}_{L}[n+1,m]\rightarrow L[n+1,m].

The total composite is contained in the saturation of (
Seg
) by
4.25
, as it is a pushout along
\Lambda^{\{1\},\{0\}}[n+1,m]\rightarrow[n+1,m]
.
By 2-out-of-3, it suffices to check that the map

\mathrm{Map}(\Gamma^{0}_{L}[n,m],\mathscr{P})\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}(\Lambda^{\{1\},\{0\}}_{L}[n+1,m],\mathscr{P})

induces an equivalence on the fibers above tuples in
C_{\mathscr{P}}^{\times(n+1)}
.
We now observe that we have a pushout square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma^{0}_{L}[n,m]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{\{1\},\{0\}}_{L}[n+1,m]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{L[n,m]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma^{0}_{L}[n+1,m].}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(d_{1})_{h}\times\mathrm{id}_{v}}

Consequently, in the induced commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}(\Gamma^{0}_{L}[n+1,m],\mathscr{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}(\Lambda^{\{1\},\{0\}}_{L}[n+1,m],\mathscr{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}(L,\mathscr{P})^{\times(n+1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}(L[n,m],\mathscr{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}(\Gamma^{0}_{L}[n,m],\mathscr{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}(L,\mathscr{P})^{\times n},}

the left square must be a pullback square. Suppose that we have a tuple
\eta\in C_{\mathscr{P}}^{\times(n+1)}
.
Then the above diagram specializes to a pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}(\Gamma^{0}_{L}[n+1,m],\mathscr{P})_{\eta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}(\Lambda^{\{1\},\{0\}}_{L}[n+1,m],\mathscr{P})_{\eta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}(L[n,m],\mathscr{P})_{\hat{\eta}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}(\Gamma^{0}_{L}[n,m],\mathscr{P})_{\hat{\eta}},}

where
\hat{\eta}
is given by the tuple
(\eta_{2},\eta_{3},\dotsc,\eta_{n+1})
.
So the desired conclusion follows from the induction hypothesis.

Secondly, we will handle the pair
(n,m+1)
. The argument is similar. In light of

4.25
, the total composite

\Lambda^{\emptyset,\{0,2\}}_{L}[n,m+1]=\Gamma^{\{0,2\}}_{L}[n,m+1]\rightarrow\Gamma^{0}_{L}[n,m+1]\rightarrow L[n,m+1]

is contained in the saturation of (
Seg
) since it can be written as a pushout along the inclusion
\Lambda^{\emptyset,\{0,2\}}[n,m+1]\rightarrow[n,m+1]
.
By 2-out-of-3, the theorem holds for the pair
(n,m+1)
whenever the map

\mathrm{Map}(\Gamma^{0}_{L}[n,m+1],\mathscr{P})\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}(\Gamma^{\{0,2\}}_{L}[n,m+1],\mathscr{P})\\

is an equivalence on the fibers above tuples in
C_{\mathscr{P}}^{\times n}
. But now we note that we have a pushout
square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma^{0}_{L}[n,m]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma^{\{0,2\}}_{L}[n,m+1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{L[n,m]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma^{0}_{L}[n,m+1].}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{h}\times(d_{2})_{v}}

Thus the desired conclusion may be inferred from the induction hypothesis again.
∎

\eta:[1,1]\rightarrow\mathscr{P}
is a 2-cell of
\mathscr{P}
that classifies a unit of a companionship. Then we have to show that the map

\mathrm{Map}([1,2],\mathscr{P})\times_{\mathrm{Map}([1,1],\mathscr{P})}\{\eta\}\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}(\Gamma^{0}[1,2],\mathscr{P})\times_{\mathrm{Map}([1,1],\mathscr{P})}\{\eta\}

is an equivalence. In turn, this translates to the condition for the map

\mathrm{Map}([1,2],\mathscr{P})\times_{\mathrm{Map}([1,1],\mathscr{P})}\{\eta\}\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}({\vtop{\halign{#\cr\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}}},\mathscr{P})\times_{\mathrm{Map}(R,\mathscr{P})}\{\eta|R\}

to be an equivalence.

Let us write
T
for the smallest bisimplicial subset of
\mathfrak{comp}
that contains the bisimplices

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1,}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1.}

The companionship data
associated to the unit
\eta
witness the existence of an extension

\eta^{\prime}:T\rightarrow\mathscr{P}

so that the composite
[1,1]\rightarrow L\rightarrow T
recovers
\eta
.
It is sufficient to demonstrate that
\mathscr{P}
is local with respect to the inclusion of bisimplicial spaces

{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}}}\cup_{R}T\rightarrow[1,2]\cup_{[1,1]}T,

because we have a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}([1,2]\cup_{[1,1]}T,\mathscr{P})\times_{\mathrm{Map}(T,\mathscr{P})}\{\eta^{\prime}\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}([1,2],\mathscr{P})\times_{\mathrm{Map}([1,1],\mathscr{P})}\{\eta\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}({\vtop{\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}}\cup_{R}T,\mathscr{P})\times_{\mathrm{Map}(T,\mathscr{P})}\{\eta^{\prime}\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}({\vtop{\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}},\mathscr{P})\times_{\mathrm{Map}(R,\mathscr{P})}\{\eta|R\},}

where the horizontal arrows are equivalences by the pasting lemma for pullback squares.
Let
L:\mathrm{PSh}(\Delta^{\times 2})\rightarrow\mathrm{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S})
denote the reflector.
Then we equivalently have to show that the induced map

i:{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}}}^{\prime}:={\vtop{\halign{#\cr\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}}}\cup_{R}LT\simeq L({\vtop{\halign{#\cr\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}}}\cup_{R}T)\rightarrow L([1,2]\cup_{[1,1]}T)\simeq[1,2]\cup_{[1,1]}LT=:[1,2]^{\prime}

constitutes an equivalence in the subcategory
\mathrm{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S})
, where both pushouts are now computed in this subcategory.

Throughout the remainder of the proof, we will exploit the language of pasting shapes that was developed in
[
Rui24b
]
.
We will make use of the
composable 2-dimensional subshape

(see Definitions 3.2 and 3.28 of
[
Rui24b
]
)
defined by

In the pictures above, we have also labeled the
vertebrae
(see
[
Rui24b
, Definition 3.5]
) of the shapes.
We will write
[-]
for the nerve functor of
[
Rui24b
, Construction 3.11]
for
2-dimensional pasting shapes.
On account of
[
Rui24b
, Theorem 3.49]
, the canonical inclusions

\displaystyle D_{0}:=[A_{1}\cup A_{4}]\cup[A_{4}\cup A_{5}]\cup[A_{23}\cup A_{6}]\rightarrow[\Sigma],

\displaystyle D_{1}:=[A_{1}\cup A_{4}]\cup[A_{2}\cup A_{4}\cup A_{5}]\cup[A_{2}\cup A_{3}\cup A_{6}]\rightarrow[2,3],

are contained in the saturation of (
Seg
). We can now write down a map of bisimplicial sets

g^{\prime}:D_{1}\rightarrow[1,2]\cup_{[1,1]}T

so that

•

g^{\prime}|[A_{1}\cup A_{4}]
selects the degenerate
(2,1)
-bisimplex

[2,1]\xrightarrow{(s_{0})_{h}\times\mathrm{id}_{v}}[1,1]\rightarrow[1,2]\cup_{[1,1]}T,

•

g^{\prime}|[A_{2}\cup A_{4}\cup A_{5}]
is the canonical map that surjects onto the image of
T
in
[1,2]\cup_{[1,1]}T
,

•

g^{\prime}|[A_{2}\cup A_{3}\cup A_{6}]
is the canonical map that surjects onto the image
[1,2]
in
[1,2]\cup_{[1,1]}T
.

Note that
g^{\prime}
restricts to a map
f^{\prime}
that fits in the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\vtop{\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}}\cup_{R}T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\Sigma]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\vtop{\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1,2]\cup_{[1,1]}T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[2,3]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1,2]^{\prime}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 8.0ptf^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip-20.0ptf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 20.0ptg^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

Here, the slanted arrows are localizations with respect to the reflector
L
. The dashed arrows in the cube hence exist and are unique (up to contractible choice).
The resulting map
g
selects the diagram in
[1,2]^{\prime}
that can be pictured as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(2,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(2,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(2,1).}

Note that there is a canonical map
[1,2]\rightarrow[\Sigma]
that factors the inclusion of the outer 2-cell in
[\Sigma]
.
By construction, the restriction

[1,1]\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}_{h}\times(d_{2})_{v}}[1,2]\rightarrow[\Sigma]\xrightarrow{f}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}}}^{\prime}

is given by the map
[1,1]\rightarrow[A_{1}\cup A_{4}]\rightarrow D_{0}\rightarrow T\rightarrow{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}}}^{\prime}
. So, the universal property of the pushout
gives rise to a unique map

r:[1,2]^{\prime}\rightarrow{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}}}^{\prime}

so that
r|[1,2]=f|[1,2]
. Our next goal is now to show that
r
is an inverse to
i
.

We will first show that
ri\simeq\mathrm{id}_{{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\lower 0.90417pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}}}^{\prime}}
. This precisely entails demonstrating that the restriction

{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}}}\rightarrow[1,2]\rightarrow[\Sigma]\xrightarrow{f}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}}}^{\prime}

is equivalent to the canonical inclusion
{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}}}\rightarrow{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}}}^{\prime}
. To this end, we note that
there is a subshape

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\quad\subset\quad\Sigma.

The composite
{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}}}\rightarrow[1,2]\rightarrow[\Sigma]
factors through the inclusion

[\Sigma^{\prime}]\subset[\Sigma]
, yielding a map

{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}}}\rightarrow[\Sigma^{\prime}]
that admits a retraction
\rho:[\Sigma^{\prime}]\rightarrow{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}}}
corresponding to the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,2).}

Note that the
spine

\mathrm{Sp}[\Sigma^{\prime}]
of

\Sigma^{\prime}
(see
[
Rui24b
, Definition 3.51]
) is contained in
D_{0}
, and moreover, the restriction
f^{\prime}|\mathrm{Sp}[\Sigma^{\prime}]
factors as the composite

\mathrm{Sp}[\Sigma^{\prime}]\xrightarrow{\rho}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}}}\rightarrow{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}}}\cup_{R}T.

On account of
[
Rui24b
, Corollary 3.52]
, the map
\mathrm{Sp}[\Sigma^{\prime}]\rightarrow[\Sigma^{\prime}]
is contained the
saturation of (
Seg
).
Hence, it must follow that the restriction
f|[\Sigma^{\prime}]
factors as the composite

[\Sigma^{\prime}]\xrightarrow{\rho}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}}}\rightarrow{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{$\sqcup$}\cr\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{$\sqcap$}\crcr}}}^{\prime}.

The desired conclusion follows from this observation.

Finally, we have to show that
ir\simeq\mathrm{id}_{[1,2]^{\prime}}
. We must demonstrate that the composite

[1,2]\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}_{h}\times(d_{2})_{v}}[1,3]\xrightarrow{(d_{1})_{h}\times\mathrm{id}_{v}}[2,3]\xrightarrow{g}[1,2]^{\prime}

is equivalent to the canonical map
[1,2]\rightarrow[1,2]^{\prime}
. To this end, we note that the image
of the spine
\mathrm{Sp}[1,3]
under the face map
{(d_{1})_{h}\times\mathrm{id}_{v}}
is contained in
{[A_{1}\cup A_{4}]}\cup[A_{2}\cup A_{5}]\cup[A_{3}\cup A_{6}]\subset D_{1}
.
Moreover, one check that the following square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sp}[1,3]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1,2]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1,2]}\cup_{[1,1]}T.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{h}\times(s_{1})_{v}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}

of bisimplicial sets commutes.
Thus the restriction
g\circ(d_{1})_{h}\times\mathrm{id}_{v}
will factor as

[1,3]\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}_{h}\times(s_{1})_{v}}[1,2]\rightarrow[1,2]^{\prime}.

This concludes the proof, since the left arrow is a retraction for
\mathrm{id}_{h}\times(d_{2})_{v}
.
∎

4.3.
Proofs of the homotopy coherence results

Now that we have
established
4.22
, we shift our focus to the proof of
4.12
.
We will construct a filtration for
\mathfrak{comp}
. Note that the
(n,m)
-bisimplices of
\mathfrak{comp}
are given by the
set
of functors
[n]\otimes[m]\rightarrow[1]

of gaunt 2-categories. Since
[1]
is a poset, these are described by maps of posets
[n]\times[m]\rightarrow[1]
.

Construction 4.26
.

Let
\sigma_{n}
be the
(n,n)
-bisimplex of
\mathfrak{comp}
that is given by the map of posets

[n]\times[n]\rightarrow[1]
so that
\sigma_{n}(i,j)=0
if
j\leq n-i
and
\sigma_{n}(i,j)=1
otherwise.
For instance,
\sigma_{2}
is described by the picture

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1.}

We will write
S_{n}\subset\mathfrak{comp}
for the smallest bisimplicial subset that contains
\sigma_{n}
. This yields a sequence of bisimplicial subsets

\textstyle L=S_{1}\subset S_{2}\subset\dotsb\subset S_{n}\subset S_{n+1}\subset\dotsb\subset\mathfrak{comp}.

Lemma 4.27
.

The inclusions
S_{i}\subset\mathfrak{comp}
exhibit that

\textstyle\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{i\in\mathbb{N}}S_{i}=\mathfrak{comp}.

Proof.

One may deduce that the canonical map
\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{i\in\mathbb{N}}S_{i}\rightarrow\bigcupop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathbb{N}}S_{i}
is an equivalence from similar considerations as in
[
Rui24b
, Remark 3.50]
.
Now one may easily conclude that the union is given by
\mathfrak{comp}
by verifying that any non-degenerate bisimplex
of
\mathfrak{comp}
can be written as an iterated face of an appropriate choice of bisimplex
\sigma_{k}
.
∎

Proof of
4.12
.

We will show the second equivalent assertion for a fixed companionship unit
\eta\in\mathscr{P}
.
In light of
4.27
, it will be sufficient to show that the map

\mathrm{Map}(S_{n+1},\mathscr{P})_{\eta}\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}(S_{n},\mathscr{P})_{\eta}

is an equivalence for every
n\geq 1
. Let
T\subset S_{n+1}
be the bisimplicial subset generated by the face
(d_{0},\mathrm{id})^{*}\sigma_{n+1}
.
Then we have a pushout squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma^{0}_{L}[n-1,n]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{L[n-1,n]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(d_{0},\mathrm{id})^{*}\sigma_{n+1}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma^{0}_{L}[n,n]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L[n,n]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{n+1},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{n+1}}

so that the desired result can be obtained from
4.22
.
∎

Remark 4.28
.

From the filtration of
4.26
one can read off what data equivalently determines (and is determined by) a companionship
unit and its associated
higher coherences that are encoded in
\mathfrak{comp}
. This yields observations that are very similar to the situation for adjunctions, cf.
[
Lur24
, Tag 02F4]
and
[
RV16
]
.

4.13
, we need the following observation:

{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.39998pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=2.33746pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -0.9 15.64 L -1.31 -8.95}{fill:none} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\pgfinnerlinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=1.5375pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -0.9 15.64 L -1.31 -8.95}{fill:none} }\lxSVG@closescope {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{-0.01675}{-0.99986}{0.99986}{-0.01675}{-0.94536pt}{-6.47078pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(-0.01675 -0.99986 0.99986 -0.01675 -1.31 -8.95)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.39998pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -1.8 3.55 C -0.92 1.68 1.42 0.07 2.76 0 C 1.42 -0.07 -0.92 -1.68 -1.8 -3.55}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

in a double Segal space
\mathscr{P}
. If
\eta
is a companionship unit, then the following assertions are equivalent:

(1)

the vertical composite
\eta^{\prime}
of
\eta
and
\alpha
is also a companionship unit,

(2)

the horizontal 2-cell
\alpha
admits an inverse
\beta
in
\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\epsilon
is a companionship counit for
\eta
.
If (1) holds, then one readily checks that the horizontal composite of
\eta
and the companionship counit for
\eta^{\prime}
is
the desired inverse
\beta
for
\alpha
.
Conversely, if (2) holds, then one readily verifies that the vertical composite
of
\beta
and
\epsilon
is a companionship counit for
\eta^{\prime}
.
∎

Proof of
4.13
.

On account of
4.12
, it suffices to show that the composite

C_{\mathscr{P}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}(L,\mathscr{P})\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}([1]_{v},\mathscr{P})

is a monomorphism.
1
1

1

A similar claim appears in the work of Gaitsgory–Rozenblyum as well
[
GR17
, Lemma 5.1.5]
, but without proof.

Equivalently, this entails demonstrating that the restriction along the fold map

L\cup_{[1]_{v}}L\rightarrow L

induces an equivalence

C_{\mathscr{P}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}(L\cup_{[1]_{v}}L,\mathscr{P})\times_{\mathrm{Map}(L,\mathscr{P})^{\times 2}}C_{\mathscr{P}}^{\times 2}.

Let
A\subset\mathrm{Sq}([1;1])
be the bisimplicial subset that is generated by the
(1,2)
-bisimplex that is pictured as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

There are two inclusions
\ell_{1}:L\rightarrow A
and
\ell_{2}:L\rightarrow A
that select
the top triangle and composite triangle in
A
respectively.
These give rise to a factorization

L\cup_{[1]_{v}}L\rightarrow A\rightarrow L

of the fold map.
Note that the first map is a pushout along
\Gamma^{0}_{L}[1,2]\rightarrow L[1,2]
. It follows from

4.22
that the inclusion
L\cup_{[1]_{v}}L\rightarrow A

induces to an equivalence

\mathrm{Map}(A,\mathscr{P})\times_{\mathrm{Map}(L,\mathscr{P})}C_{\mathscr{P}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{Map}(L\cup_{[1]_{v}}L,\mathscr{P})\times_{\mathrm{Map}(L,\mathscr{P})}C_{\mathscr{P}}

after base changing via
\ell_{1}
. We may now base change via
\ell_{2}
as well,
so that we obtain an equivalence

D:=\mathrm{Map}(A,\mathscr{P})\times_{\mathrm{Map}(L,\mathscr{P})^{\times 2}}C_{\mathscr{P}}^{\times 2}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{Map}(L\cup_{[1]_{v}}L,\mathscr{P})\times_{\mathrm{Map}(L,\mathscr{P})^{\times 2}}C_{\mathscr{P}}^{\times 2}.

Since monomorphisms are closed under base change,
D
maybe described as the subspace
of
\mathrm{Map}(A,\mathscr{P})
spanned by the maps
f:A\rightarrow\mathscr{P}
so that the restrictions

f\ell_{1}
and
f\ell_{2}
are companionship units. On account of
4.29
, these two conditions for
f

are equivalent to conditions that
f\ell_{1}
is a companionship unit and the restriction
f|[1;1]_{h}
is an invertible 2-cell.

We will now use the fact that the restriction map

\mathrm{Map}([1;J]_{h},\mathscr{P})\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}([1;1]_{h},\mathscr{P})

is a monomorphism
onto the horizontal 2-cells that admit an inverse. This can be deduced from applying
[
Rez01
, Theorem 6.2]
to the restricted Segal space
\mathrm{Hor}(\mathscr{P})_{1,\bullet}
.
Combining this with the above description of
D
, it follows that the canonical map

A\rightarrow A\cup_{[1;1]_{h}}[1;J]_{h}

induces an equivalence

\mathrm{Map}(A\cup_{[1;1]_{h}}[1;J]_{h},\mathscr{P})\times_{\mathrm{Map}(L,\mathscr{P})}C_{\mathscr{P}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}D,

where the pullback in the domain is formed via
\ell_{1}
.
Now, we note that we an obvious commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L\cup_{[1]_{h}}[1;1]_{h}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L\cup_{[1]_{h}}[1;J]_{h}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L\cup_{[1]_{v}}L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\cup_{[1;1]_{h}}[1;J]_{h}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}

where the bottom arrows compose to the fold map.
The square that appears in the diagram is a pushout square, and the left vertical arrow is contained in the saturation of (
Seg
). Hence, the right vertical arrow is contained in the saturation of (
Seg
) as well.
The slanted arrow is contained in the saturation of (
lhc
). Consequently, the map

C_{\mathscr{P}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}(L\cup_{[1]_{v}}L,\mathscr{P})\times_{\mathrm{Map}(L,\mathscr{P})^{\times 2}}C_{\mathscr{P}}^{\times 2}
decomposes into equivalences

\displaystyle C_{\mathscr{P}}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{Map}(A\cup_{[1;1]_{h}}[1;J]_{h},\mathscr{P})\times_{\mathrm{Map}(L,\mathscr{P})}C_{\mathscr{P}}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\simeq}D

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{Map}(L\cup_{[1]_{v}}L,\mathscr{P})\times_{\mathrm{Map}(L,\mathscr{P})^{\times 2}}C_{\mathscr{P}}^{\times 2}.\qed

Remark 4.30
.

Suppose that
f
is a vertical arrow in a double Segal space
\mathscr{P}
(without any completeness assumptions)
that admits a companion
F
. Then the proof of
4.13
shows that the fiber of the map

C_{\mathscr{P}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S})}([1]_{v},\mathscr{P})

above
f
is equivalent to the space of automorphisms

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S})}([1;J]_{h},\mathscr{P})\times_{\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S})}([1]_{h},\mathscr{P})}\{F\}.

5.
Companions and conjoints in functor double Segal spaces

Our next goal is to identity the companions and conjoints in functor double Segal spaces
using the machinery of
4
.

5.1.
Companionable and conjointable 2-cells

To
give the characterization of the companions and conjoints in functor double Segal spaces, we introduce the following double categorical variant on
adjointable squares:

in
\mathscr{P}

is said to be
companionable
if
F
and
G
are the companions
of arrows
f:a\rightarrow b
and
g:c\rightarrow d
respectively, so that the vertical 2-cell
kf\rightarrow gh
given by the pasting

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\scriptstyle\alpha}

constitutes an equivalence in
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})(a,d)
. Here the top and bottom 2-cell are the companionship unit and counit respectively.

Dually, we say that
\alpha
is
conjointable
if
F
and
G
are the conjoints
of arrows
f:b\rightarrow a
and
g:d\rightarrow c
respectively, and so that the similarly obtained vertical 2-cell
gk\rightarrow hf
is invertible in

\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})(b,c)
.

Example 5.2
.

Let us consider the locally complete double Segal space
\mathrm{Sq}(X)
of squares in an locally complete
2-fold Segal space
X
(see
3.59
).
Let
\alpha
be a 2-cell in
\mathrm{Sq}(X)
that corresponds to a lax commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}

in
X
. One may verify that
\alpha
is companionable if and only if
\alpha
strictly commutes, i.e. the
2-cell filling the square is invertible. From the above, one can deduce that
\alpha
is conjointable if and only if
F
and
G
admit left adjoints
f
and
g

respectively, so that the associated mate

gk\rightarrow gkFf\rightarrow gGhf\rightarrow hf

is an equivalence in
X(b,c)
.
In the terminology of Lurie,
\alpha
is thus conjointable if and only if it is
left adjointable

[
Lur09a
, Definition 7.3.1.2]
.

There is also another characterization of companionable squares:

Proposition 5.3
.

Suppose that

\alpha=\hbox to73.22pt{\vbox to51.24pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 36.61182pt\lower-26.59299pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

in the space of 2-cells
\mathscr{P}_{1,1}
. Here the top left and bottom right 2-cells are companionship units.

Proof.

It is clear that (2) implies (1) in light of the companionship triangle identities. Conversely, suppose that (1) holds.
Then we can expand the pasting that appears on the left in (2) as

Here the bottom right 2-cell is the companionship counit for the pair
(g,G)
. Since
\alpha
is companionable,
the right column of the right hand-side pastes to an invertible 2-cell in
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})
. By assumption,

\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})
is locally complete, hence this column must be equivalent to the identity 2-cell on the vertical arrow
gh
. Thus we deduce that

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

as desired.
∎

Using the language of companionable 2-cells, we can phrase the main theorem of this section:

Theorem 5.4
.

Let
X
and
\mathscr{P}
be a bisimplicial space and locally complete double Segal space respectively.
Suppose that
\alpha:h\rightarrow k
is a horizontal natural transformation between functors
h,k:X\rightarrow\mathscr{P}
.
Then the following assertions are equivalent:

(1)

\alpha
is a companion in
\mathbb{F}\mathrm{un}(X,\mathscr{P})
,

(2)

for every vertical arrow
f:x\rightarrow y
in
X
, the associated naturality 2-cell

\alpha|(f\times\mathrm{id}_{[1]_{h}})=\hbox to103.87pt{\vbox to53.87pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 52.00389pt\lower-27.35132pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

is companionable in
\mathscr{P}
.

If these equivalent conditions are met, then the vertical natural
transformation
\beta:h\rightarrow k
in
\mathbb{F}\mathrm{un}(X,\mathscr{P})
whose companion is given by
\alpha
, admits the following description.
For every vertical arrow
f:x\rightarrow y
in
X
, the commutative naturality square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{y}}

in
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})
corresponds to the pasting in
5.1
associated to the 2-cell in (2).

Remark 5.5
.

There is a dual statement for conjoints which can be formally obtained from the above.

Corollary 5.6
.

Let
\mathscr{P}
be a locally complete double Segal space. Suppose that
X

is a 2-fold Segal space and let
\alpha:h\rightarrow k
be a horizontal natural transformation in the
horizontal cotensor product
\{X,\mathscr{P}\}
.
Then the following assertions are equivalent:

(1)

\alpha
is a companion,

(2)

for each
x\in X
, the component horizontal arrow

\alpha_{x}:h(x)\rightarrow k(x)

is a companion in
\mathscr{P}
.

Proof.

It is clear that (1) implies (2). Conversely, suppose that (2) holds. In light of
5.4
,
we have to show that the naturality 2-cell of
\alpha
associated with any vertical arrow
f
of
X_{h}
, is companionable. These
naturality 2-cells must be horizontal identity 2-cells, since every
f:[1]_{v}\rightarrow X_{h}
factors through the degeneracy map
[1]_{v}\rightarrow[0]_{v}
. The companionability
requirement thus translates to condition (2).
∎

Corollary 5.7
.

The following classes of locally complete double Segal spaces are closed
under vertical cotensor products:

(1)

the ones with all companions of vertical arrows,

(2)

the ones with all conjoints of vertical arrows.

Remark 5.8
.

We apply
5.4
in
[
Rui24a
]
to obtain a characterization
of companions and conjoints in the double
\infty
-categories of
\infty
-categories internal to
\infty
-toposes.

5.2.
Proof of the main result

We will proceed
corepresentably

in our demonstration of
5.4
. We will make use of

4.13
which
allows us reduce the proof to the cases that
X=[1]_{h}
and
X=[1]_{v}
via a descent argument. These instances can then be tackled using explicit combinatorics.

The first substantial input is the following characterization of companionship units in functor double Segal space:

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
X
and
\mathscr{P}
be a bisimplicial space and double Segal space respectively. Then a map
\eta:L\rightarrow\mathbb{F}\mathrm{un}(X,\mathscr{P})
classifies a companionship unit if and only if
each restriction

\eta_{x}:L\rightarrow\mathbb{F}\mathrm{un}(X,\mathscr{P})\xrightarrow{\{x\}^{*}}\mathscr{P}

classifies a companionship unit in
\mathscr{P}
for every
x\in X
.

Proof.

We must show that
{\eta}
lies in the image of the restriction monomorphism

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S})}(\mathfrak{comp},\mathbb{F}\mathrm{un}(X,\mathscr{P}))\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S})}(L,\mathbb{F}\mathrm{un}(X,\mathscr{P}))

on account of
4.12
.
We can write
X
as a colimit of grids,

X\simeq\mathop{colim}\displaylimits_{[n,m]\rightarrow X\in\Delta^{\times 2}/X}[n,m],

so that we may reduce to the case that
X=[n,m]
. In turn, such a grid may canonically be decomposed as a colimit
of free 0-cells, 1-cells and 2-cells. This allows us to reduce to the case that
n,m\in\{0,1\}
. Using the
fact that
[1,1]
decomposes as the product

[1,1]=[1,0]\times[0,1]=[1]_{h}\times[1]_{v},

one can verify that the instance
X=[1,1]

follows from the cases
X=[1]_{h}
and
X=[1]_{v}
. We
may further reduce to the single case
X=[1]_{v}
by using the transpose duality.

We will write
V
and
S
for the smallest bisimplicial subsets of
\mathfrak{comp}
containing the bisimplices

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1,}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

respectively. An extension of
\eta
to a map
S\rightarrow\mathbb{F}\mathrm{un}(X,\mathscr{P})
would witness that
\eta

is a companionship unit, since
S
encodes a companionship counit and triangle identities (with extra coherences).
We will equivalently demonstrate that the adjunct map

\eta^{\sharp}:[1]_{v}\times L\rightarrow\mathscr{P}
extends to a map
[1]_{v}\times S\rightarrow\mathscr{P}
. In fact, we will end up showing that this extension
is essentially unique.
Suppose that
[1]_{v}\times L\subset A\subset B\subset[1]_{v}\times\mathfrak{comp}
are bisimplicial subsets. For the sake of this argument,
we will say that the inclusion
A\rightarrow B
is
good
if it induces an equivalence on fibers

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{PSh}(\Delta^{\times 2})}(B,\mathscr{P})_{\eta}\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{PSh}(\Delta^{\times 2})}(A,\mathscr{P})_{\eta}.

We will show that all inclusions

[1]_{v}\times L\rightarrow[1]_{v}\times V\rightarrow[1]_{v}\times S

are good, and this will then prove the lemma.

Let us start by handling the first inclusion. We will consider the inclusion

[1]_{v}\times L\rightarrow[1]_{v}\times L\cup_{\{0,1\}_{v}\times L}\{0,1\}_{v}\times V=:A.

The inclusion
L\rightarrow V
is a pushout along
\Lambda^{0}_{L}[1,2]\rightarrow L[1,2]
(see
4.21
).
Hence, it follows from
4.22
and the fact that
\eta_{0}
and
\eta_{1}
are companionship units that this inclusion is good.
By 2-out-of-3, it is now
necessary and sufficient to show that the inclusion
A\rightarrow[1]_{v}\times V

is good.
Note that
A
is precisely
the smallest bisimplicial subset of
[1]_{v}\times\mathfrak{comp}
containing the (1,2)-bisimplices

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,1),}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,1).}

Let us define
B\subset[1]_{v}\times V
to be the smallest bisimplicial subset containing
A
and the
(1,2)-bisimplex

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,1).}

It is readily verified that the inclusion
A\rightarrow B
is a pushout along
\Gamma^{0}_{L}[1,2]\rightarrow L[1,2]
. Using
4.22
once again,
we deduce that
it suffices to check that
B\rightarrow[1]_{v}\times V
is good.
Now we use that

Let
C_{i}
denote the smallest bisimplicial subset of
[1]_{v}\times V
containing
B
and
\sigma_{1},\dotsc,\sigma_{i}
.
This yields a finite filtration

B=C_{0}\rightarrow C_{1}\rightarrow C_{2}\rightarrow C_{3}=[1]_{v}\times V.

On account of
4.25
and
4.22
, each inclusion
is good:
the first inclusion is a pushout along

\Lambda^{\emptyset,1}[1,3]\rightarrow[1,3]
, and the second and third maps are pushouts along
\Gamma^{0}_{L}[1,3]\rightarrow L[1,3]
.

Finally, we show that the map

[1]_{v}\times V\rightarrow[1]_{v}\times S

is good. This demonstration is similar. We will first consider the inclusion

[1]_{v}\times V\rightarrow[1]_{v}\times V\cup_{\{0,1\}_{v}\times V}\{0,1\}_{v}\times S=:D.

The inclusion
V\rightarrow S
is a pushout along the map
\Gamma^{0}_{L}[2,2]\rightarrow L[2,2]
, so that we can use

4.22
to conclude that the above inclusion is good.
Hence, it suffices to show that the inclusion
D\rightarrow[1]_{v}\times S
is good.
Let
E
be the smallest bisimplicial subset of
[1]_{v}\times\mathfrak{comp}
containing
D
and the (3,3)-bisimplex

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,1).}

Then
D\rightarrow E
is a pushout along
\Gamma^{0}_{L}[3,3]\rightarrow L[3,3]
, so it suffices
to check that
E\rightarrow[1]_{v}\times S
is good by
4.22
. Again, we filter this as follows. Consider
the generating (2,3)-bisimplices of
[1]_{v}\times S
:

\tau_{1}:=\hbox to135.61pt{\vbox to82.78pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 67.8056pt\lower-41.38887pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-67.8056pt}{-35.22914pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -93.82 -48.75)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}

Let
F_{i}
denote the smallest bisimplicial subset of
[1]_{v}\times S
containing
E
and
\tau_{1},\dotsc,\tau_{i}
.
Then we obtain a filtration

E=F_{0}\rightarrow F_{1}\rightarrow F_{2}\rightarrow F_{3}=[1]_{v}\times S,

so that each map is good: the first map is a pushout along

\Lambda^{\emptyset,1}[2,3]\rightarrow[2,3]
and the second and third maps are pushouts along
\Gamma^{0}_{L}[2,3]\rightarrow L[2,3]
.
∎

Construction 5.10
.

We
consider the double Segal space

Q:=[1]_{v}\times[1]_{h}\cup_{\{0,1\}_{v}\times[1]_{h}}\{0,1\}_{v}\times L,

induced by the map
[1]_{h}\rightarrow L
that selects the non-degenerate horizontal arrow. This pushout is computed in
\mathrm{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S})
. It comes with a canonical functor

q:Q\rightarrow[1]_{v}\times L.

Now there are two 2-cells

c_{1},c_{2}:[1,1]\rightarrow Q

that select the pastings

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,1)}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,1),}

respectively.

One may readily verify that the constructed pastings
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
are co-equalized by
q
in
[1]_{v}\times L
, i.e. there is a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1,1]}\cup_{\Lambda^{1,\emptyset}[1,1]}{[1,1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1,1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1]}_{v}\times L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(c_{1},c_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

of double Segal spaces.
In fact, a crucial observation is that
[1]_{v}\times L
is universal with regard to this property:

Lemma 5.11
.

The above square is a pushout square of double Segal spaces.

Proof.

Let us consider the following diagram of bisimplicial spaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1,1]^{\sqcup 2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1,2]}^{\sqcup 2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1,1]}\cup_{\Lambda^{1,\emptyset}[1,1]}[1,1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1,2]}\cup_{\Lambda^{1,\emptyset}[1,1]}[1,2]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1,1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1]_{v}\times[1,1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1]}_{v}\times L,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[1,d_{1}]^{\sqcup 2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(c_{1}^{\prime},c_{2}^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

where all pushouts are computed in
\mathrm{PSh}(\Delta^{\times 2})
.
Here
c_{1}^{\prime}
and
c_{2}^{\prime}
are the evident extensions of
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
.
The total square of the left column is a pushout square in
\mathrm{PSh}(\Delta^{\times 2})
, which comes
from the decomposition

[1]_{v}\times[1,1]\simeq[1]_{h}\times[1]_{v}\times[1]_{v}\simeq[1]_{h}\times([2]_{v}\cup_{[1]_{v}}[2]_{v}).

The top left square is a pushout square as well. Hence, the bottom left square is a pushout square.
Consequently, the lemma holds if the right bottom square is carried to a pushout square by the reflector

\mathrm{PSh}(\Delta^{\times 2})\rightarrow\mathrm{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S})
.

Consider the
bisimplicial set

Q^{\prime}:=[1]_{v}\times[1]_{h}\cup_{\{0,1\}_{v}\times[1]_{h}}\{0,1\}_{v}\times L\subset[1]_{v}\times L,

where the pushout is now computed in
\mathrm{PSh}(\Delta^{\times 2})
.
It comes with a canonical map
Q^{\prime}\rightarrow Q
that is contained in the saturation of (
Seg
). The right bottom square of the above diagram
fits in a further commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{1,1}[1,2]\cup_{\Lambda^{1,\emptyset}[1,1]}\Lambda^{1,1}[1,2]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1,2]}\cup_{\Lambda^{1,\emptyset}[1,1]}[1,2]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1]_{v}}\times[1,1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1]}_{v}\times L.}

One readily verifies that the outer square is a pushout square in
\mathrm{PSh}(\Delta^{\times 2})
.
The desired result now follows from the abservation that the top left horizontal arrow is contained in the saturation of
(
Seg
) on account of
4.25
.
∎

We will now treat the final ingredient for the proof of
5.4
.

Construction 5.12
.

We will make use of the bisimplicial subset

\textstyle P:={[1]_{h}}\times[1]_{h}\bigcupop\displaylimits_{{\{0,1\}_{h}\times[1]_{h}}}\{0,1\}_{h}\times L

of
[1]_{h}\times L
. Here, the pushout is computed in
\mathrm{PSh}(\Delta^{\times 2})
.

Lemma 5.13
.

Suppose that
\eta_{0}
and
\eta_{1}
are companionship units in a double Segal space
\mathscr{P}
.
Then the inclusion
P\rightarrow[1]_{h}\times L
induces an equivalence on fibers

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{PSh}(\Delta^{\times 2})}([1]_{h}\times L,\mathscr{P})\times_{\mathrm{Map}(\{0,1\}_{h}\times L,\mathscr{P})}\{(\eta_{0},\eta_{1})\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{PSh}(\Delta^{\times 2})}(P,\mathscr{P})\times_{\mathrm{Map}(\{0,1\}_{h}\times L,\mathscr{P})}\{(\eta_{0},\eta_{1})\}.}

Proof.

Note that
[1]_{h}\times L
is generated
by the bisimplices

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,1),}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,1).}

We can attach the first bisimplex to
P
by pushing out along
\Lambda^{1,\emptyset}[2,1]\rightarrow[2,1]
. Then we may attach
the second bisimplex to the resulting bisimplicial space via a pushout along
\Gamma^{0}_{L}[1,2]^{\mathrm{t}}\rightarrow L[1,2]^{\mathrm{t}}
.
So, the desired result follows from
4.25
and
4.22
.
∎

Proof of
5.4
.

Suppose that (1) holds so that we obtain an extension

\bar{\alpha}:X\times\mathfrak{comp}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}

of
\alpha
. For any vertical arrow
f
in
X
, we can consider the two restrictions

[1,1]\overset{c_{1}}{\underset{c_{1}}{\rightrightarrows}}Q\xrightarrow{q}[1]_{v}\times L\xrightarrow{f\times\mathrm{incl}}X\times\mathfrak{comp}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}.

We already observed at the start of this subsection that

c_{1}
and
c_{2}
are co-equalized by
q
, hence the two restrictions are equivalent.
Thus the naturality 2-cell
\alpha
for
f
is companionable on account of
characterization (2) of
5.3
.

Conversely, suppose that (2) holds. Then we must show that
{\alpha}
lies in the image of the restriction monomorphism

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S})}(\mathfrak{comp},\mathbb{F}\mathrm{un}(X,\mathscr{P}))\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Cat}^{2}(\mathscr{S})}([1]_{h},\mathbb{F}\mathrm{un}(X,\mathscr{P}))

on account of
4.13
. We may apply the same reasoning as in the proof of
5.9

to reduce to the cases that
X=[1]_{h}
and
X=[1]_{v}
.
On account of
4.12
and
5.9
, we must show that in these cases
a dotted extension in the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times[1]_{h}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\alpha}}

exists so that the restriction of
\bar{\alpha}
to
\{x\}\times L
classifies a companionship unit for each
x\in X
.

Consider the case that
X=[1]_{v}
. Then
\alpha
is the datum of a companionable 2-cell in
\mathscr{P}
. The associated companionship units
yield an extension
\alpha^{\prime}:Q\rightarrow\mathscr{P}
of
\alpha
. In light of characterization (2)
of
5.3
,
the companionability of
\alpha
bears witnesses to the co-equalization of
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
by
\alpha^{\prime}
, so that
5.11
implies that the desired extension
\bar{\alpha}
exists. The case that

X=[1]_{h}
is covered by
5.13
.
∎

6.
Application: double
\infty
-categories of
(\infty,2)
-functors

The goal of this section is to give an application of
5.4
to
(\infty,2)
-category theory.
Given two
(\infty,2)
-categories
\mathscr{X}
and
\mathscr{Y}
, we will first show that one can construct a double
\infty
-category

\mathbb{F}\mathrm{un}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y})

with:

•

the objects given by functors
\mathscr{X}\rightarrow\mathscr{Y}
,

•

the vertical arrows given by natural transformations between functors
\mathscr{X}\rightarrow\mathscr{Y}
,

•

the horizontal arrows given by
lax
natural transformations between functors
\mathscr{X}\rightarrow\mathscr{Y}
,

as a particular vertical cotensor product. We will then show that one can use
5.4

to recover Haugseng’s
recognition theorem for adjunctions in
(\infty,2)
-categories
of functors and lax natural transformations
[
Hau21
, Theorem 4.6]
, for a particular
choice of model for the Gray tensor product (cf.
6.3
).

6.1.
The Gray tensor product

There is a wide array of different approaches to defining the Gray tensor product for
(\infty,2)
-categories. A nice
summary of the different models can be found in the introduction of
[
CM23
]
. Since we have developed enough theory, we can give a brief and reasonably self-contained treatment of a model of this tensor product using double
\infty
-categorical technology
that was proposed by Gaitsgory and Rozenblyum
[
GR17
, Section 10.4.5]
. The following is justified by

3.59
:

Definition 6.1
.

The
Gray tensor product
of
(\infty,2)
-categories
\mathscr{X}
and
\mathscr{Y}
is defined
to be the (essentially) unique
(\infty,2)
-category
\mathscr{X}\otimes\mathscr{Y}
with the property that

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}}(\mathscr{X}\otimes\mathscr{Y},\mathscr{Z})\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{DblCat}_{\infty}^{c}}(\mathscr{X}_{h}\times\mathscr{Y}_{v},\mathrm{Sq}(\mathscr{Z}))

naturally for all
\mathscr{Z}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}
.

Note that the above definition is functorial in both variables so that we obtain a functor

(-)\otimes(-):\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}\times\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}\rightarrow\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}.

Remark 6.2
.

It directly follows from these definitions that the Gray tensor product preserves colimits in each variable and that

[n]\otimes[m]\in\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}
agrees with the strict Gray tensor product of
[n]
and
[m]
for all
[n],[m]\in\Delta
.

Remark 6.3
.

This definition of the Gray tensor product has not yet been compared to other constructions
of Gray tensor products that appear in the literature.

However, we can say something about its restriction to
(\infty,1)
-categories:
the restricted bifunctor
(-)\otimes(-):\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}\times\mathrm{Cat}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathrm{Cat}_{(\infty,2)}
is the unique
one that preserves
colimits in each variable and restricts to the strict Gray tensor product on
\Delta\times\Delta
. In light of
[
HHLN21
, Proposition 5.1.9]
,
the Gray tensor product of Gagna, Harpaz and Lanari
[
GHL21
]
satisfies these conditions, thus it coincides with
(-)\otimes(-)
when restricted
to
(\infty,1)
-categories.

To compare with other Gray tensor products in full generality, one can readily compute the values of
[1;1]\otimes[1]
and
[1;1]\otimes[1;1]
in terms of certain
pushouts by using the description
[1;1]_{h}=[0,1]^{\sqcup 2}\cup_{[0,0]^{\sqcup 2}}[1,1]
. This yields fundamental formulas where the only Gray tensor products appearing are reduced to the form
([1]\times[1])\otimes[1]
and
([1]\times[1])\otimes([1]\times[1])
.
If these formulas are true for other Gray tensor products, this allows you to reduce to the above case since
every
(\infty,2)
-category can be written as a canonical colimit of the free cells
[0]
,
[1]
and
[1;1]
.

Definition 6.4
.

Let
\mathscr{X}
and
\mathscr{Y}
be two
(\infty,2)
-categories. We define the
double
\infty
-category
of functors from
\mathscr{X}
to
\mathscr{Y}
with lax natural transformations
as the vertical cotensor product

\mathbb{F}\mathrm{un}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y}):=[\mathscr{X},\mathrm{Sq}(\mathscr{Y})].

The horizontal arrows of this double
\infty
-category correspond precisely to functors
[1]\otimes\mathscr{X}\rightarrow\mathscr{Y}
and these are called
lax natural transformations
.

Proposition 6.5
.

Let
\mathscr{X}
and
\mathscr{Y}
be
(\infty,2)
-categories, then:

(1)

\mathbb{F}\mathrm{un}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y})
is complete and admits all companions,

(2)

there is a natural equivalence
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathbb{F}\mathrm{un}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y}))\simeq\mathrm{FUN}(\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y})
,

(3)

a lax natural transformation
\alpha:h\rightarrow k
is a companion if and only if
for every arrow
x\rightarrow y
in
\mathscr{X}
, the associated lax square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{y}}

commutes, i.e. the 2-cell filling the square is invertible.

Proof.

Note that (1) follows from
3.44
and
5.7
. Assertion (2)
follows from
3.50
and (3) can be deduced from
5.2
and
5.4
.
∎

Definition 6.6
.

We will write

\mathrm{Fun}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y})\text{ and }\mathrm{FUN}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y})

for the horizontal
\infty
- and
(\infty,2)
-categorical fragments of
\mathbb{F}\mathrm{un}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y})
respectively.

6.2.
Lax adjunctions between functors

We can now give a double categorical proof of Haugseng’s
recognition theorem for adjunctions in
(\infty,2)
-categories
of functors and lax natural transformations
[
Hau21
, Theorem 4.6]
:

Theorem 6.7
.

Let
v:k\rightarrow h
be a lax natural transformation between functors
k,h:\mathscr{X}\rightarrow\mathscr{Y}
. Then the
following assertions are equivalent:

(1)

v
is a right adjoint in
\mathrm{FUN}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y})
,

(2)

v
is a conjoint in
\mathbb{F}\mathrm{un}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y})
,

(3)

for any arrow
x\rightarrow y
in
\mathscr{X}
, the horizontal morphisms in the lax square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{y}}

in
\mathscr{Y}
admit left adjoints and the associated mate is an equivalence (cf.
5.2
).

If these equivalent conditions are met, the left adjoint
u
of
v
is strict and for any arrow
x\rightarrow y
in
\mathscr{X}
, the horizontal morphisms in the
commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{y}}

are given by left adjoints to
v_{x}
and
v_{y}
, and its commutativity is witnessed by the mate of the square in (2).

We will need the following lemma:

Lemma 6.8
.

Let
\mathscr{P}
be a locally complete double Segal space that admits all companions.
Suppose that
X
is a 2-fold Segal space and
v:k\rightarrow h
is a horizontal arrow
in
[X,\mathscr{P}]
.
Then the following assertions are equivalent:

(1)

v
is a conjoint,

(2)

v
is a right adjoint in
\mathrm{Hor}([X,\mathscr{P}])
and
v_{x}:k(x)\rightarrow h(x)
is a conjoint in
\mathscr{P}
for all
x\in X
.

If these equivalent conditions are met, then the
left adjoint of
v
is given by the companion of the vertical arrow
in
[X,\mathscr{P}]
whose conjoint is
v
.

Proof.

Note that
[X,\mathscr{P}]
again admits all companions on account of
5.7
.
If (1) holds, then the left adjoint to
v
exists and is given by the companion
of the vertical arrow of
[X,\mathscr{P}]
whose conjoint is
v
, see
4.8
. Clearly,
each component
v_{x}
is also a conjoint in this case.

Conversely, let us assume that (2) holds. Suppose that
u:h\rightarrow k

is the left adjoint of
v
and denote the unit and counit
of the adjunction
(u,v)
by
\eta
and
\epsilon
respectively. Moreover,
let us write
f_{x}:h(x)\rightarrow k(x)
for the vertical arrow in
\mathscr{P}
whose conjoint is
v_{x}
for each
x\in X
.
Then it must follow that
u_{x}
is the companion of
f_{x}
by
4.8
and unicity of left adjoints. Moreover,

\eta(x)
and
\epsilon(x)
can be identified with the horizontal pastings of the companion and conjoint unit, and companion and conjoint counit,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(x),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{x}}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(x),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{x}}

respectively. To show that
v
is a conjoint,
5.4
implies that
it suffices to show
that for any arrow
f:x\rightarrow y
in
X
, the pastings

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(y),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{y}}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(y),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{y}}

are inverses to each other in
\mathrm{Vert}(\mathscr{P})(h(x),k(y))
.
This is now readily verified using the coherence and above pointwise decompositions of
\eta
and
\epsilon
, and the companionship and conjunction identities.
∎

Proof of
6.7
.

The equivalence of (1) and (2) follows from
6.8
and the fact that all horizontal arrows in
\mathrm{Sq}(\mathscr{Y})
are companions.
In turn, the equivalence of the assertions (2) and (3) follows from
5.4
and
5.2
. The final claim
in the theorem statement follows from

5.4
as well.
∎

Corollary 6.9
.

Let
u:h\rightarrow k
be a lax natural transformation between functors
h,k:\mathscr{X}\rightarrow\mathscr{Y}
. Then the following assertions are equivalent:

(1)

u
is a left adjoint in
\mathrm{FUN}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y})
,

(2)

u
is strict and admits a conjoint in
\mathbb{F}\mathrm{un}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y})
,

(3)

u
is strict and each component
u_{x}:h(x)\rightarrow k(x)
is a left adjoint in
\mathscr{Y}
.

If these equivalent conditions are met, then for any arrow
x\rightarrow y
in
\mathscr{X}
, the horizontal morphisms in the
lax naturality square of the right adjoint
v

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{y}}

are given by left adjoints to
v_{x}
and
v_{y}
, and the filling 2-cell is given by the mate of the corresponding
naturality square of
u
.

Remark 6.10
.

A first complete proof of
6.7
, albeit using a different definition of the Gray tensor product, was
recently given by Abellán, Gagna and Haugseng
[
AGH24
]
.

Remark 6.11
.

One readily verifies that
6.7
and
6.9
also go through whenever
\mathscr{X}\in\mathrm{PSh}(\Delta^{\times 2})_{\mathrm{deg}}
, and
\mathscr{Y}
is a locally complete 2-fold Segal space. In this case, one can still
define
\mathbb{F}\mathrm{un}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y}):=[\mathscr{X},\mathrm{Sq}(\mathscr{Y})]
, which will now be a locally complete double Segal space.

*
